e\mathbin{:}R\mathit{iface}(R)\hskip 9.24994ptm[\overline{\beta~S^{\prime\prime}}](\overline{y\>\Phi})\>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{T}@{V}}\mathbin{\in}\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(R)\neg\mathit{tyvar}_{\Delta}(T[\eta])\hskip 9.24994pt\eta=(\overline{\beta\mathbin{:=}S})\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mapsto e^{\prime}\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash\overline{d:U}\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash\overline{d\mathbin{:}U\Mapsto_{\Phi[\eta]}d^{\prime}}}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e.m[\overline{S}](\overline{d})\mapsto e^{\prime}.m_{D}[\overline{{\lfloor S\rfloor}}](\overline{d^{\prime}}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\lfloor T[\eta]\rfloor}@{\lfloor T[\eta]\rfloor})}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Call${}_{I}$}\par\vskip-3.0pt\frac{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mathbin{:}R\mathit{iface}(R)\hskip 9.24994ptm[\overline{\beta~S^{\prime\prime}}](\overline{y\>\Phi})\>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{T}@{V}}\mathbin{\in}\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(R)\mathit{tyvar}_{\Delta}(T[\eta])\hskip 9.24994pt\eta=(\overline{\beta\mathbin{:=}S})\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mapsto e^{\prime}\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash\overline{d:U}\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash\overline{d\mathbin{:}U\Mapsto_{\Phi[\eta]}d^{\prime}}}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e.m[\overline{S}](\overline{d})\mapsto e^{\prime}.m_{D}[\overline{\lfloor S\rfloor}](\overline{d^{\prime}}).({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{\lfloor T[\eta]\rfloor}}\rightarrow\lfloor{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}T[\eta]}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}@}{V[\eta]}\rfloor)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Call${}_{I\alpha}$}

Figure 16.
Compilation rules: expressions (part 2)

This example reveals a fundamental challenge in the uniform
compilation of methods in the presence of structural typing: When
compiling a method body we cannot
a priori
predict which
interfaces the type will implement and so we cannot locally determine
what arguments must be boxed or not (e.g. method
process
in

IntProcessor
and in
GenericProcessor
). Dually, when
considering the call site to an interface method (e.g., the call to

process
in
useProcessor
) we also cannot statically
determine the type of the method receiver and so we cannot predict
whether arguments and return values need to be boxed by inspecting the
signature of the interface.
We can manifest the issue further by considering an additional
interface

type

SimpleProcessor

interface

\{
process
(
x

int
)
int
\}
. We have that both
IntProcessor
and

GenericProcessor
[
int
]
implement
SimpleProcessor
and
any context performing a call to
process
on a value of type

SimpleProcessor
has no way of determining if the receiver
expects boxing (i.e.,
GenericProcessor
) or not
(i.e.
IntProcessor
).

Adaptor Methods

Our solution to this issue is to compile
two
versions of each
method
m
: One whose signature (and name) is identical to the source
WG signature and whose body is compiled accordingly; and a

dynamic adaptor
version
m_{D}
of the method, that expects
all

arguments and return value to be boxed. The body of
m_{D}

redirects to
m
with the appropriate unboxing operations.

We first address the compilation of method calls. The rules are given
in Figure
16
.
The rules are split into two categories, depending on whether the call
is on an interface (i.e., a
dynamic
call) or on a non-interface
(i.e., a
static
call): Rule
Call
I
and Rule
Call
Iα
deal with the former, and the two
Call
S

rules deal with the latter.
Two rules per category are required to deal with potential unboxing of
the value returned by the call.

In the static call cases, the arguments are compiled according to
their WG signature, via the synthetic cast judgement. If the return
type of
m
is boxed then we unbox it (
Call
S
-boxed
)
since the calling context expects an unboxed result; otherwise, we
leave it unchanged (
Call
S
-unboxed
).

The compilation of dynamic calls boxes all arguments, as needed by the
adaptor (the
\Mapsto
judgement boxes all values, see
Figure
16
).
As for the return type, we check whether it is a type variable in
WG. If so, then the method call happens in a context where the return
type is still parametric and must therefore be boxed according to the
bound of the type variable.
We will see that the adaptor method
m_{D}
returns a boxed value with
an
empty
method table (the bound information is only determined
at the call site). Thus, we
change
the type accordingly, so that
the appropriate method table is generated (rule
Call
Iα
).
If the return type is
not
a type variable, then we type assert
the result to the return type. This has the effect of unboxing the
result if needed by the calling context.

Revisiting our initial example, the call to
process
in the
body of
useProcessor
compiles to a dynamic call to the adaptor
for
process
, boxing the integer argument and unboxing the
return value, since the return of
process
in

Processor
[
int
]
is not a type variable, Rule
Call
I
applies, we obtain:

\texttt{processor.process}_{D}(\texttt{int}(42).({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\texttt{int}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\texttt{int}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}@}{\texttt{any}})).({\texttt{int}}@{\texttt{int}})
.

The compilation of method declarations is given in
Figure
17
. Rule
d-func
generates two methods per
WG method
m
.
Method
m
is the compiled version of the original method, with an identical signature (modulo
type compilation) and with method body obtained by compiling the
source body.
Adaptor method
m_{D}
bridges dynamic method call destined to
m
.
Its signature is
obtained by forcing the types of arguments and the return type to be
boxed—achieved by the auxiliary function
\langle\Phi\rangle
.

The adaptor method body consists of a (static) call to method
m
,
generated by an auxiliary
\mathit{Adapt}
function that inspects the WG
signature of
m
to determine which arguments need to be unboxed and
whether the return value of
m
needs to be boxed.
To ensure the adaptor returns a boxed result, we insert a make
instruction with an empty method table via the synthetic cast

({\lfloor V\rfloor}\rightarrow_{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{\lfloor{V}@{\texttt{Any}}\rfloor}})
when required.
Finally, Rule
d-type
compiles type declarations. Its sole
purpose is to apply the
\lfloor\Phi\rfloor
transformation on all
(anonymous) interfaces it may contain.

Compilation: declarations
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash D\mapsto\overline{D}

\frac{mM=m[\overline{\beta~S}](\overline{y~\Phi})~\Psi\overline{\Phi}=\overline{{T}@{T^{\prime}}}\Delta=\overline{\alpha:R},\overline{\beta:S}\Gamma=x:t[\overline{\alpha}],\overline{y:T}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{R\mapsto R^{\prime}}\overline{S\mapsto S^{\prime}}\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mathbin{:}V\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mathbin{:}V\mapsto_{\Psi}e^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptD={\texttt{\bf func}~(x~t[\overline{\alpha~R^{\prime}}])~m_{D}[\overline{\beta~S^{\prime}}](\overline{y~\lfloor\langle\Phi\rangle\rfloor})~\lfloor\langle\Psi\rangle\rfloor~\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\,\texttt{\bf return}~\textit{Adapt}((x~t[\overline{\alpha~R}])~mM)\,\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}{{\texttt{\bf func}~(x~t[\overline{\alpha~R}])~mM~\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\bf return}~e\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\mapsto\{D,\ \texttt{\bf func}~(x~t[\overline{\alpha~{R^{\prime}}}])~m[\overline{\beta~S^{\prime}}](\overline{y~\lfloor\Phi\rfloor})~\lfloor\Psi\rfloor~\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\bf V\rfloor}\rightarrow_{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{\lfloor{V}@{\texttt{Any}}\rfloor}})}\par\frac{\overline{R\mapsto R^{\prime}}T\mapsto T^{\prime}}{\texttt{\bf Figure 17.
Compilation rules: method declarations

5.3.
Properties of Compilation

Compilation of a well-typed WG program results in a well-typed LWG
program that preserves the behaviour of the original WG program.
The former property is embodied in Theorem
5.2
, which
states that a well-typed WG expression of type
T
compiles to a
well-typed LWG expression of type
\lfloor T\rfloor@\lfloor\mathit{bounds}_{\Delta}(T)\rfloor
, presupposing all declarations in the ambient
program are compiled. The resulting type captures the essence of our
typing scheme for LWG, where expressions are assigned a (logical) type
but also track the type bound.

Theorem 5.2.

If
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T
and
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e\mapsto e^{\prime}
then
\lfloor\Delta\rfloor;\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor_{\Delta}\vdash e^{\prime}:\lfloor T\rfloor@\lfloor\mathit{bounds}_{\Delta}(T)\rfloor
.

Before stating our behavioural correspondence result, we define how WG
and LWG programs should be related. All definitions introduced here
are given formally in Section
9.4
.

There is a lowering simulation between a WG program
\overline{D}\rhd e
and
an LWG program
\overline{D^{\prime}}\rhd e^{\prime}
, if whenever
e\longrightarrow d

then
e^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{\ast}d^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}
are in a lowering
simulation. Also, if
e
is a value, then
d
is an
equivalent

value; and if
e
is stuck, then so is
d
.
A lifting simulation is the reverse, e.g., if
d
can make a move,
then
e
should be able to match it, etc.
The theorem below relies on relation
\bumpeq
(a weak
bisimulation) on LWG expressions and which essentially adds or removes
synthetic casts.

Theorem 5.3.

Suppose
\overline{D}\rhd e~\mathit{\checkmark}
, then:

\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\,(e,d^{\prime})\,\mid\,\exists d.\ \emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e\mapsto d\text{ and }d\bumpeq d^{\prime}\,\}

is a lowering simulation; and

\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\,(d,e)\,\mid\,\exists d^{\prime}.\ \emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e\mapsto d^{\prime}\text{ and }d\bumpeq d^{\prime}\,\}

is a lifting simulation.

6.
Related Work

This work fits within the established tradition of programming
language research that studies formal models of real-world languages,
e.g.,
(
Griesemer et al. 2020
;
Igarashi et al. 2001
;
Grigore 2017
;
Amin and Rompf 2017
;
Kennedy and Syme 2001
;
Jung et al. 2018
;
Park et al. 2015
)
.

Formal investigations of Go.

Griesemer et al. 2020
presented the first
core formalism of Go in FGG (Featherweight Generic Go), modelling
generic types and structural subtyping.
WG was initially motivated by an investigation into the type safety of the
features of type unions and type sets.
This quickly led to the observation that FGG’s core subset fails to
characterise significant semantic features of Go.
By relying on nominal typing of structs and omitting underlying and
anonymous types, FGG does not accurately model Go’s structural typing
that allows methods to accept differently-named structs with the same
underlying structure.
FGG relies solely on interface-driven subtyping, which does not
capture Go’s full structural typing nor expose the subtleties in
different type coercions: assignability (a non-transitive superset of
interface subtyping), static conversions (safe no-ops), and dynamic
assertions (requiring RTTI).

Various approaches to implementing Go have been studied based on FGG.

Griesemer et al. 2020
formalised monomorphisation as a
translation from FGG to non-generic FG.

Ellis et al. 2022
presented an approach based on
translating FGG to non-generic FG using structs to implement RTTI dictionaries
for generic type arguments.

Sulzmann and Wehr 2023
presented an approach of translating FGG
sans dynamic type assertions (and RTTI) to an
untyped

\lambda
-calculus similar to that known from Haskell type classes.
All these remain within FGG, and thus do not consider the features that we
have introduced in WG.
None consider the underlying mechanics of the Go runtime, such as interface
values, RTTI, runtime type conversions and static versus dynamic resolution of
operations, as in LWG.
In LWG, we distinguish concrete runtime items (e.g., RTTI)
from ghost entities used by our metatheory to establish our correctness
properties.
Note that LWG actions and rules that operate solely on ghost entities
correspond to no-ops in an actual implementation.

Regarding correctness,
Griesemer et al. 2020
shows FGG
programs and their FG monomorphisations are bisimilar.

Ellis et al. 2022
show their FGG to FG translation is a
bisimulation up to dictionary resolution.

Sulzmann and Wehr 2023
show their (untyped)
\lambda
-calculus
translation is value and divergence preserving.
By contrast, we establish a behavioural equivalence
between high level WG and compilations into low level LWG.
The challenges we address in this paper relate to establishing
safety for LWG and the correspondence between high and low level
behaviours.

Implementations of generics.

Various approaches have been used to implement generics in OO languages with
differing implications and trade-offs.
Java employs an erasure approach
(
Bracha et al. 1998
)
where generic type
information is discarded by the compiler, which restricts programs from
performing operations that may dynamically depend on such.
C# employs a mixed approach
(
Yu et al. 2004
;
Kennedy and Syme 2001
)
where generic code
for object types is generated once statically, supported by RTTI at runtime,
but generic code for primitives types is specialised dynamically by JIT
compilation.
The type theoretic study of RTTI for polymorphism
dates back to
Harper and Morrisett 1995
and
Crary et al. 1998
.
Rust employs static
monomorphisation
(
Turon 2015
;
The Rust Team 2025
)
, enabling
zero-cost abstractions at the cost of output code size and compilation time.
All use nominal subtyping, and do not tackle the interplay between structural
subtyping, generics and non-uniform representations (interfaces vs. constants/structs) as in Go.

As discussed earlier, Go employs static
monomorphisation
(
Griesemer et al. 2020
)
and
runtime dictionary passing
(
Randall 2022
)
. The Go compiler
monomorphises code up to GC shapes with RTTI for lost type information
to balance specialisation against code size.
This incurs compilation costs and
restrictions such as disallowing polymorphic recursion.
This paper instead combines Go’s runtime infrastructure for type
conversions with statically generated adapter code, incurring more
runtime conversions but supporting all of WG, including proposed
features like generic methods, while lifting restrictions and
maintaining separate compilation compatibility.

Leroy 1992
presented an approach of boxing and unboxing
generic function arguments and results for ML, with careful treatment of
higher order functions.
It does not consider subtyping and the language disallows polymorphic
recursion.
By contrast, our work tackles the problem of compiling generic code in the
presence of structural subtyping between non-uniform representations.

In future work, we will consider techniques to optimise our approach, such
as combining our adaptors with localised monomorphisation of package private
code, and statically safe elimination of type
conversions
(
Leroy 1992
)
.
The overhead of runtime assembly of runtime type-reps can be optimised by
pre-computing and caching maps from open types to their reps when a generic
type or method is instantiated
(
Viroli and Natali 2000
)
.

Acknowledgements.

We would like to thank Ian Lance Taylor for valuable discussions,
and the anonymous reviewers for their feedback.
This work was supported by national funds through Fundação para a Ciência
e a Tecnologia, I.P. (FCT) under projects UID/50021/2025 and
UID/PRR/50021/2025.

Data-Availability Statement

We have implemented a minimal prototype of WG and LWG in Go as interpreters,
and our compilation approach as a translation from WG to LWG. The
interpreters perform type checking of programs and reduction of their main
expressions. Our software artifact is available on Zenodo
(
Hu et al. 2025
)
.
It also includes the examples from this paper as tests.

9.
Proofs

9.1.
WG Type Safety

Lemma 9.1 (Weakening).

Let
\alpha\not\in\mathit{dom}(\Delta)
and
x\not\in\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
:

(1)

If
\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}
then
\Delta,\alpha:S\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}

(2)

If
\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
then
\Delta,\alpha:S\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}

(3)

If
\Delta\vdash\overline{\beta\,T}~\mathit{\checkmark}
then
\Delta,\alpha:S\vdash\overline{\beta\,T}~\mathit{\checkmark}

(4)

If
\Delta\vdash m[\overline{\beta~T}](\overline{x~U})~U~\mathit{\checkmark}
then

\Delta,\alpha:S\vdash m[\overline{\beta~T}](\overline{x~U})~U~\mathit{\checkmark}

(5)

If
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T
then
\Delta,\alpha:S;\Gamma\vdash e:T
and
\Delta;\Gamma,x:S\vdash e:T

(6)

If
\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\Delta\vdash U~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}U
implies
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta,\alpha:S}T^{\prime}

(7)

If
\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\Delta\vdash U~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}U
implies
T\prec_{\Delta,\alpha:S}T^{\prime}

Proof.

Each statement above is proved by a straightforward induction on the
given derivation.
∎

Lemma 9.2.

If
\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\Delta\vdash S~\mathit{\checkmark}
then:

(1)

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T

(2)

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}S
and
S\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}U
implies
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}U

(3)

T\prec_{\Delta}T

Proof.

Statements (1) and (3) above follow straightforwardly from the
definitions of implements and assignable, respectively, with
reflexivity of assignability relying on reflexivity of implements.

Statement (2) follows by induction on the given derivations:

Case::

\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{T}
– Impossible due to

\neg\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(\alpha)
.

Case::

\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
– Immediate by
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
.

Case::

\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{T}
and
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\alpha}
– Impossible.

Case::

\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{T}
and
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
– Immediate by
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
.

Case::

\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
and
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\alpha}
– Impossible.

Case::

\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
and
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{T}
– Impossible.

Case::

\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
and
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}

\mathit{types}_{\Delta}(S)\subseteq\mathit{types}_{\Delta}(T)

\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(S)\supseteq\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(T)

\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(T)

\neg\mathit{tyvar}_{\Delta}(T)
by inversion

\mathit{types}_{\Delta}(T)\subseteq\mathit{types}_{\Delta}(U)

\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(T)\supseteq\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(U)

\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(U)

\neg\mathit{tyvar}_{\Delta}(U)
by inversion

\mathit{types}_{\Delta}(S)\subseteq\mathit{types}_{\Delta}(U)
by
transitivity of
\subseteq

\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(S)\supseteq\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(U)
by
transitivity of
\supseteq

S\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}U
by
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}

∎

Lemma 9.3 (Quasi-transitivity).

Let
\Delta\vdash T,T^{\prime},S~\mathit{\checkmark}
. If
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}\prec:_{\Delta}S
then
T\prec:_{\Delta}S
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the given derivations.

Case::

alpha\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}alpha

Subcase:

\alpha\prec:S
from
\alpha\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}S

Follows by transitivity of
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}

No other possible derivation of
\prec:

Case::

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}T
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(T)

Immediate since
T=T^{\prime}

Case::

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}T^{\prime}
from
\mathit{iface}(T^{\prime})
,
\mathit{types}(T)\subseteq\mathit{types}(T^{\prime})
,
\mathit{methods}(T)\supseteq\mathit{methods}(T^{\prime})
,
\neg\mathit{tyvar}(T^{\prime})

Subcase:

T^{\prime}\prec:S
from
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}S

Follows by transitivity of
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}

Subcase:

T^{\prime}\prec:S
from
\mathit{under}(T^{\prime})=S

Since
\mathit{iface}(T^{\prime})
then
S=\mathit{iface}{\dots}

\mathit{methods}(S)=\mathit{methods}(T^{\prime})
and
\mathit{types}(S)=\mathit{types}(T^{\prime})

therefore

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}S
and so
T\prec:S
.

Subcase:

T^{\prime}=L
and
L\prec:t[\dots]
with
L=\mathit{under}(t[\dots])

Since
T^{\prime}=L
then
\mathit{iface}(L)
and so
\mathit{iface}(t[\dots])

and
so
methods(L)=methods(t[\dots])
and
types(L)=types(t[\dots])
and

therefore
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}t[\dots]
and so
T\prec:t[\dots]

∎

Lemma 9.4.

If
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}S
then
\mathit{types}(T)\subseteq\mathit{types}(S)
and
\mathit{methods}(T)\supseteq\mathit{methods}(S)
.

Proof.

Straightforward by definition.
∎

Lemma 9.5 (Type Substitution preserves Subtyping).

If
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:U},\Delta_{1}}S
,
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\overline{U_{0}}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}\overline{U}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
then
T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]}S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:U},\Delta_{1}}S
.

When the derivation is by
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\alpha}
or
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{T}
the result
follows immediately. When the derivation is by
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{I}
, we have that

S
is a non-type variable interface type,
\mathit{types}(T)\subseteq\mathit{types}(S)
and
\mathit{methods}(T)\supseteq\mathit{methods}(S)
and both properties
are preserved under substitution.
∎

Lemma 9.6 (Type Substitution preserves Assignability).

If
T\prec_{\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:U},\Delta_{1}}:S
,
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\overline{U_{0}}\prec_{\Delta_{0}}\overline{U}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
then
T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]\prec_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}:[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]}S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
.

Proof.

If assignability is derived by rule
\prec_{\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.60275pt}{{:}}}}
then the result
follows by Lemma
9.5
; if derived by rule
\prec_{tL}

or
\prec_{Lt}
then the result is straightforward.
∎

Lemma 9.7 (Bounds commutes with type substitution).

If
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}\vdash S~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\overline{U}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}\overline{T}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]
with
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}
then
\mathit{bounds}_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]}(S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}])\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]}(\mathit{bounds}_{\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}}(S))[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]

Proof.

By case analysis on the implements relation and the derivation of
S~\mathit{\checkmark}
.
∎

Lemma 9.8 (Type substitution preserves well-formedness (1)).

If
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}\vdash S~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\overline{U}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]
with
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]\vdash S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]~\mathit{\checkmark}

Proof.

Straightforward induction on the derivation of
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}\vdash S~\mathit{\checkmark}
, appealing to
Lemmas
9.5
and
9.7
.
∎

Lemma 9.9 (Type substitution preserves well-formedness (2)).

If
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}\vdash S~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
and
\overline{U}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]
with
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]\vdash S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}

Proof.

By Lemma
9.8

∎

Lemma 9.10 (Type Substitution preserves typing).

If
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1};\Gamma\vdash e:S
and

\overline{U}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]
with
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}];\Gamma[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]\vdash e[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]:S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]

Proof.

By induction on typing. Most cases follow immediately by the
induction hypothesis and the corresponding typing rule.
Let
\eta=(\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U})
and
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}=\Delta
:

Case::

t-field
(
e=e.f_{i}
)

\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1};\Gamma\vdash e:S^{\prime}

\mathit{struct}(S^{\prime})

(f\,\overline{\Phi})=\mathit{fields}_{\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}}(S^{\prime})

by inversion

(f\,\overline{\Phi}[\eta])=\mathit{fields}_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta]}(S^{\prime}[\eta])

by definition

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta]:S^{\prime}[\eta]

by i.h.

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].f_{i}:\Phi_{i}[\eta]\downharpoonright_{1}

by
t-field

Case::

t-call
(
e=e.m[S^{\prime}](\overline{e})

\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}=\Delta
)

\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1};\Gamma\vdash e:R

\Delta\vdash\overline{S^{\prime}}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}

m[\overline{\alpha^{\prime}\,S}][\overline{x\,\Phi}]\,\Psi\in\mathit{methods}(R)

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\overline{e:T^{\prime}}

\eta^{\prime}=(\overline{\alpha^{\prime}:=S^{\prime}})

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}[\eta^{\prime}]\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}S[\eta^{\prime}]}

\overline{T^{\prime}[\eta^{\prime}]\prec:_{\Delta}\Phi\downharpoonright_{1}[\eta^{\prime}]}

by inversion

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta]:R[\eta]
by
i.h.

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash\overline{e[\eta]:T^{\prime}[\eta]}
by i.h.

m[\overline{\alpha^{\prime}\,S[\eta]}][\overline{x\,\Phi[\eta]}]\,\Psi[\eta]\in\mathit{methods}(R[\eta])
by def.

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta]\vdash\overline{S^{\prime}[\eta]}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
by
Lemma
9.9

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}[\eta^{\prime}][\eta]\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta]}S[\eta^{\prime}][\eta]}
by Lemma
9.5

\overline{T^{\prime}[\eta^{\prime}][\eta]\prec:_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta]}\Phi\downharpoonright_{1}[\eta^{\prime}][\eta]}

by Lemma
9.6

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].m[\overline{S^{\prime}[\eta]}](\overline{e[\eta]}):\Psi\downharpoonright_{1}[\eta^{\prime}][\eta]
by
t-call

Case::

t-op-param

\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}=\Delta

\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1};\Gamma\vdash\overline{e}:\beta

\beta:T^{\prime}\in\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}

\{\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(V)\mid V\in\mathit{types}(T^{\prime})\}\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\circ)

by inversion

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash\overline{e[\eta]}:\beta[\eta]
by i.h.

if
\beta\not\in\overline{\alpha}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta]\vdash\circ(\overline{e[\eta]}):\beta
by
t-op-param

if
\beta\in\overline{\alpha}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash\overline{e[\eta]}:U_{i}
with
U_{i}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}T^{\prime}

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta]\vdash\circ(\overline{e[\eta]}):U_{i}
by
t-op

Case::

t-assert
I

\mathit{iface}(T)

\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:S

\mathit{iface}(S)
by inversion

\Delta_{0};\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta]:S[\eta]
by
i.h.

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta]\vdash T[\eta]~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
by
Lemma
9.9

if
T\neq\alpha_{i}
and
S=\alpha_{j}
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(S[\eta])

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-stupid

if
T\neq\alpha_{i}
and
S=\alpha_{j}
and
\mathit{iface}(S[\eta])

then

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-assert
I

if
T\neq\alpha_{i}
and
S\neq\alpha_{j}

then

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-assert
I

if
T=\alpha_{i}
and
S\neq\alpha_{j}
and
\mathit{iface}(T[\eta])

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-assert
I

if
T=\alpha_{i}
and
S\neq\alpha_{j}
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(T[\eta])

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-stupid
or
t-assert-s

if
T=\alpha_{i}
and
S=\alpha_{j}
and
\mathit{iface}(T[\eta])

and
\mathit{iface}(S[\eta])
then

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-assert-i

if
T=\alpha_{i}
and
S=\alpha_{j}
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(T[\eta])

and
\mathit{iface}(S[\eta])
then

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-assert-s

if
T=\alpha_{i}
and
S=\alpha_{j}
and
\mathit{iface}(T[\eta])

and
\neg\mathit{iface}(S[\eta])
then

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-stupid

if
T=\alpha_{i}
and
S=\alpha_{j}
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(T[\eta])

and
\neg\mathit{iface}(S[\eta])
then

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]

by
t-stupid

Case::

t-assert
S

\neg\mathit{iface}(T)

\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:S

\mathit{iface}(S)

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}\mathit{bounds}_{\Delta}(S)
by inversion

\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e:S[\eta]
by i.h.

if
S\neq\alpha_{i}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]
by

S
and

9.7

if

then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\eta];\Gamma[\eta]\vdash e[\eta].(T[\eta]):T[\eta]
by

or

∎

Lemma 9.11 (Substitution preserves Typing).

If
\Delta;\Gamma,\overline{x:T}\vdash e:S
and
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\overline{e:T}
then
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e[\overline{x}:=\overline{e}]:S
.

Proof.

By induction on typing of
\Delta;\Gamma,\overline{x:T}\vdash e:S
. All cases are standard.
∎

Lemma 9.12.

If
(m[\overline{\alpha~S}](\overline{x~T})~T)\in\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(U)
and

\mathit{body}(U.m[\overline{S^{\prime}}])=(x,\overline{x}).e_{0}
with

\Delta\vdash U~\mathit{\checkmark}
,

\Delta\vdash\overline{S^{\prime}}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
,

\overline{S^{\prime}}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}\overline{S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{S^{\prime}}]}
then there exists

T^{\prime}
such that
\Delta\vdash T^{\prime}~\mathit{\checkmark}
,
T^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{S^{\prime}}]
and

\Delta;x:U,\overline{x:T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{S^{\prime}}]}\vdash e_{0}:T^{\prime}

Proof.

By inversion on
\mathit{body}
and well-formedness, followed by
Lemmas
9.5
and
9.10
.
∎

Theorem 9.13 (Type Preservation).

If
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T
and
e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}

then
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:S
, for some
S
such that
S\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T

Proof.

By induction on the reduction relation
e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
.
We show illustrative cases.

Case::

r-assert

v.(T)\longrightarrow v
with
\mathit{type}(v)\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}T
this case

Subcase:

\vdash v.(T):T
by
t-assert
S

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash v:T^{\prime}

\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(T^{\prime})

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}\mathit{bounds}(T^{\prime})

by inversion

\mathit{type}(v)=T^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}T

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash v.(T):T
by
t-assert
I

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash v:T^{\prime}

\mathit{iface}(T^{\prime})

by inversion

\mathit{type}(v)=T^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}T

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash v.(T):T
by
t-stupid

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash v:T^{\prime}

by inversion

\mathit{type}(v)=T^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T

Case::

e.(T)\longrightarrow e^{\prime}.(T)

By inversion on typing we have 3 subcases.

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e.(T):T
by
t-assert
I

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T^{\prime}
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:S^{\prime}
with
S^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T^{\prime}
by i.h.

if
\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(S^{\prime})
then
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}.(T):T
by
t-assert
I

if
\neg\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(S^{\prime})
then
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}.(T):T
by
t-stupid

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e.(T):T
by
t-assert
S

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T^{\prime}

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T^{\prime}
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:S^{\prime}
with
S^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T^{\prime}
by i.h.

if
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}S^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}.(T):T
by
t-assert
S

if
T\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}$\cr$/$\crcr}}}_{\Delta}S^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}.(T):T
by
t-stupid

if
\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(S^{\prime})
then
S^{\prime}=T^{\prime}
by value restriction and so

T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}S^{\prime}

thus,
e^{\prime}.(T):T
by
t-assert
S

if
\neg\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(S^{\prime})
then

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}.(T):T
by
t-stupid

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e.(T):T
by
t-stupid

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:S^{\prime}
with
S^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T^{\prime}
by i.h.

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}.(T):T
by
t-stupid

Case::

r-convert-b

T(U(c))\longrightarrow T(c)
with
\mathit{base}(T)
this case

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(U(c)):T
by inversion on
t-conv-a
or
t-conv-u

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash U(c):U
by
t-const
with
\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(U)=\mathit{ctype}(c)

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(c):T

by
t-const
since

\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(T)=\mathit{ctype}(c)
by
t-conv-u

and
U\prec:_{\Delta}T

implies
\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(T)=\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(U)

Case::

r-convert-s

T(U\{\overline{v}\})\longrightarrow T\{\overline{v}\}
with
\mathit{struct}_{\Delta}(T)

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(U\{\overline{v}\}):T
by
t-conv-a
or
t-conv-u

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(U\{\overline{v}\}):T
by
t-conv-a

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash U\{\overline{v}\}:U
and
U\prec:_{\Delta}T
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\overline{v:S}
and
(\overline{f\,\Phi})=\mathit{fields}_{\Delta}(U)
and
\overline{S\prec:_{\Delta}\Phi\downharpoonright_{1}}
by inversion

\mathit{fields}_{\Delta}(U)=\mathit{fields}_{\Delta}(T)
since
U\prec:_{\Delta}T

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T\{\overline{v}\}:T
by
t-literal

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(U\{\overline{v}\}):T
by
t-conv-u

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash U\{\overline{v}\}:U
and
\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(U)=\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(T)

by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\overline{v:S}
and
(\overline{f\,\Phi})=\mathit{fields}_{\Delta}(U)
and
\overline{S\prec:_{\Delta}\Phi\downharpoonright_{1}}
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T\{\overline{v}\}:T
by
t-literal

Case::

T(e)\longrightarrow T(e^{\prime})
by
r-context

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(e):T
by
t-conv-a

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:U
and
U\prec:_{\Delta}T
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:U^{\prime}
with
U^{\prime}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}U
by i.h.

U^{\prime}\prec:_{\Delta}T
by Lemma
9.3

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(e^{\prime}):T
by
t-conv-a

Subcase:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(e):T
by
t-conv-u

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:U
and
\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(U)=\mathit{under}_{\Delta}(T)
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:U^{\prime}
with

by i.h.

by
\prec:

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(e):T
by
t-conv-a

if

by inversion on

by assumption

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash T(e):T
by
t-conv-u

\mathit{iface}_{\Delta}(T)
by inversion

Lemma 9.14 (WG Canonical Forms).

If
e
is a value and
\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash e:T
then either

T
is a struct type and
e=T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, for some
\overline{v}
or

T
is a base type and
e=T(c)
, for some
c
.

Proof.

Straightforward induction on typing.
∎

Theorem 9.15 (WG Progress).

If
\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash e:T
then either
e
is a value,
e\,\mathsf{panic}
or
e\rightarrow e^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on typing. The proof is essentially identical to that of
FGG
(
Griesemer et al. 2020
)
.
We detail the cases pertaining to constants and type conversions.

Case::

t-assert
I

\vdash e.(T):T
this case

\vdash e:S
and
\mathit{iface}(S)
by inversion

e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
or
e
is a value or
e\,\mathsf{panic}
by
i.h.

If
e
reduces or panics the result is trivial

Subcase:

e
is a value

e=w
for some
w
by Lemma
9.14

if
\mathit{type}(w)\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}T
then we conclude by
r-assert

otherwise then
e.(T)\,\mathsf{panic}
.

Other type assert cases follow identical reasoning.

Case::

t-const

Trivial since constants are values.

Case::

t-conv-a

\vdash T(e):T
with
\vdash e:U
and
U\prec:T
by
inversion

e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
,
e
is a value or
e\,\mathsf{panic}
by
i.h.

e
reducing or panic are trivial.

Subcase:

e
is a value

if
\mathit{base}(T)
then we conclude by
r-convert-b

if
\mathit{struct}(T)
then we conclude by
r-convert-s

if
\mathit{iface}(T)
then we conclude by
r-convert-i

∎

9.2.
LWG Type Safety

Lemma 9.16 (Quasi-transitivity).

Let
\Delta\vdash T,T^{\prime},S~\mathit{\checkmark}
. If
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}\prec:_{\Delta}S
then
T\prec:_{\Delta}S
.

Proof.

Identical to Lemma
9.3
since the relations are the same.
∎

Lemma 9.17 (Type Substitution preserves Subtyping).

If
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:U},\Delta_{1}}S
,
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\overline{U_{0}}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}\overline{U}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
then
T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]}S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
.

Proof.

Identical to Lemma
9.5
since the relations are the same.
∎

Lemma 9.18 (Type Substitution preserves Assignability).

If
T\prec_{\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:U},\Delta_{1}}:S
,
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\overline{U_{0}}\prec_{\Delta_{0}}\overline{U}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
then
T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]\prec_{\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}:[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]}S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U_{0}}]
.

Proof.

Identical to Lemma
9.18
since the relations are the same.
∎

Lemma 9.19 (Type substitution preserves well-formedness (1)).

If
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}\vdash S~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\overline{U}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]
with
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]\vdash S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]~\mathit{\checkmark}

Proof.

Identical to Lemma
9.8
since the relations are the same.
∎

Lemma 9.20 (Type substitution preserves well-formedness (2)).

If
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1}\vdash S~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
and
\overline{U}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]
with
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]\vdash S[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}

Proof.

By Lemma
9.19

∎

Lemma 9.21 (Type Substitution preserves Typing).

If
\Delta_{0},\overline{\alpha:T},\Delta_{1};\Gamma\vdash e:S@V
and

\overline{U}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta_{0}}T[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]
with
\Delta_{0}\vdash\overline{U}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
then
\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}];\Gamma[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]\vdash e[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]:(S@V)[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{U}]

Proof.

By induction on the given typing derivation. Similar to the analogue
property for WG.
∎

Lemma 9.22 (Substitution preserves Typing).

If
\Delta;\Gamma,\overline{x:T@V}\vdash e:S@U
and
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\overline{e:T@V}
then
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e[\overline{x}:=\overline{e}]:S@U
.

Proof.

Straightforward induction, similar to the analogue
property for WG.
∎

Lemma 9.23.

If
(m[\overline{\beta~S}](\overline{y~\Phi})~\Psi)\in\mathit{methods}_{\Delta}(t[\overline{R}])
and

\mathit{body_{L}}(t.m)=[\overline{\alpha};\overline{\beta}].(x,\overline{x}).e_{0}
with

\Delta\vdash\overline{S^{\prime}}~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
,

\overline{S^{\prime}}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}\overline{S[\overline{\beta}:=\overline{S^{\prime}}]}
then

\Delta;x:t[\overline{R}]@t[\overline{R}],\overline{x:\Phi[\overline{\beta:=S^{\prime}}]}\vdash e_{0}[\overline{\alpha:=R}][\overline{\beta:=S^{\prime}}]:\Psi[\overline{\beta:=S^{\prime}}]

Proof.

By inversion on
\mathit{body_{L}}(t.m)
, Lemmas
9.21
and
9.17
.
∎

Theorem 9.24 (Type Preservation).

If
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T@T^{\prime}
and
e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}

then
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T@T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on the reduction relation.

Case::

select

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash S\{\overline{v}\}.i:S_{i}@V_{i}
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash S\{\overline{v}\}:T@T
and
\mathit{struct}_{\Delta}(T)
and
\mathit{fields}_{\Delta}(T)=(\overline{\_\,S@V})
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\overline{v:S@V}
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash v_{i}:S_{i}@V_{i}
as needed.

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash U@V(S,\rho,w):U@V

t-interface
and weakening

by inversion on
typing

\rho=\mathsf{mkTable}_{\Delta}(S,V)

U=S
by inversion

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash U@V(S,\rho,w):U@V
by

t-interface
and weakening

\Phi(S,\rho,w).(T\rightarrow T^{\prime}@U)\longrightarrow T^{\prime}@U(S,\rho^{\prime},w)

\rho^{\prime}=\mathsf{mkTable}_{\emptyset}(S,V)

Lemma 9.25 (LWG Canonical Forms).

If
e
is a value and
\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash e:T@V
then either:

•

\mathit{iface}(V)
and
e=T@V(S,\rho,w)
for some
S,\rho,w
.

•

\neg\mathit{iface}(V)
and
T=V
with
\mathit{struct}(T)
and
e=T\{\overline{v}\}
, for some
\overline{v}
or
\mathit{base}(T)
and
e=T(c)

for some
c
.

Proof.

Straightforward induction on typing.
∎

Theorem 9.26 (LWG Progress).

If
\vdash e:T@V
then
e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
,
e
is a value or
e\,\mathsf{panic}
.

Proof.

By induction on typing. We show a few cases:

Case::

t-make

\vdash e.({T}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}@{V}}):U@V
this case

\vdash e:T@T
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(T)
by inversion

e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
,
e
is a value or
e\,\mathsf{panic}
by
i.h.

Reduction and panic cases are trivial.

e
is a value:

e=w
for some
w
by Lemma
9.25

We conclude by reduction
make
accordingly.

Case::

t-change

\vdash e.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}T}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}U}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}@}{V^{\prime}}):U@V^{\prime}
by
inversion

\vdash e:T@V
by inversion

e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
,
e
is a value or
e\,\mathsf{panic}
by
i.h.

Reduction and panic cases are trivial.

e
is a value:

Since
\mathit{iface}(V)
,
e=T@V(S,\rho,w)
by
Lemma
9.25

We conclude by reduction
change-type
accordingly.

Case::

t-static-change

\vdash e.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}T}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}U}):U@U
this case

\vdash e:T@T
by inversion

\neg\mathit{iface}(T,U)
and
\mathit{under}(T)=\mathit{under}(U)

by inversion

e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
,
e
is a value or
e\,\mathsf{panic}
by
i.h.

Reduction and panic cases are trivial.

e
is a value:

Since
\neg\mathit{iface}(T,U)
,
e=w
by
Lemma
9.25

We conclude by reductions
static-change-s
or
static-change-c
accordingly.

Case::

t-dyn-call

Standard, noting that
\mathit{iface}(V)
provides us with the
appropriate information for Lemma
9.25
in the
appropriate subcase, where
e
equals some
R@V(t[\overline{U}],\rho,w)
, allowing
us to conclude
\rho(m)=t.m
and
\overline{\alpha};\,\overline{\beta}](x,\overline{y}).e=\mathit{body_{L}}(\rho(m))
.

Other cases are straightforward.

∎

9.3.
Compilation

We use the following auxiliary definitions below:

\frac{}{\lfloor\Delta;\Gamma\rfloor\triangleq\lfloor\Delta\rfloor;\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor_{\Delta}}\par\frac{}{\lfloor\Delta,\alpha:T\rfloor\triangleq\lfloor\Delta\rfloor,\alpha:\lfloor T\rfloor}\par\frac{}{\lfloor\Gamma,x:T\rfloor_{\Delta}\triangleq\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor_{\Delta},x:\lfloor T\rfloor@\lfloor\mathit{bounds}_{\Delta}(T)\rfloor}

Lemma 9.27 (Compilation Preserves Well-formedness).

•

\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}
implies
\lfloor\Delta\rfloor\vdash\lfloor T\rfloor~\mathit{\checkmark}

•

\Delta\vdash T~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}
implies
\lfloor\Delta\rfloor\vdash\lfloor T\rfloor~\mathit{\checkmark}_{\!\text{b}}

Proof.

Straightforward induction on definition of compilation of types.
∎

Lemma 9.28 (Compilation Preserves Implements and Assignability).

•

If
T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}U
then
\lfloor T\rfloor\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\lfloor\Delta\rfloor}\lfloor U\rfloor

•

If
T\prec:_{\Delta}U
then
\lfloor T\rfloor\prec:_{\lfloor\Delta\rfloor}\lfloor U\rfloor

•

if
\mathit{under}(U)=\mathit{under}(T)
then

\mathit{under}(\lfloor T\rfloor)=\mathit{under}(\lfloor U\rfloor

Proof.

Straightforward induction on definition of compilation of types.
∎

Theorem 9.29 (Compilation Preserves Types).

If
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T
:

•

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e\mapsto e^{\prime}
implies
\lfloor\Delta;\Gamma\rfloor\vdash e^{\prime}:\lfloor T\rfloor@\lfloor\mathit{bounds}_{\Delta}(T)\rfloor
.

•

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e:T\mapsto_{U@V}e^{\prime}
and
T\prec:U
and (
\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
implies
U=T
) or (
\neg\mathit{iface}(T)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
and

\mathit{under}(T)=\mathit{under}(U)
) then

\lfloor\Delta;\Gamma\rfloor\vdash e^{\prime}:U@V
.

•

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\mathbin{:}U\Mapsto_{{T}@{V}}d^{\prime}
and
T\prec:U
and (
\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
implies
U=T
) or (
\neg\mathit{iface}(T)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
and

\mathit{under}(T)=\mathit{under}(U)
) and
\neg\mathit{iface}(V)
implies

\lfloor\Delta;\Gamma\rfloor\vdash e^{\prime}:\lfloor T@\mathsf{Any}\rfloor

•

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\mathbin{:}U\Mapsto_{{T}@{V}}d^{\prime}
and
T\prec:U
and (
\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
implies
U=T
) or (
\neg\mathit{iface}(T)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
and

\mathit{under}(T)=\mathit{under}(U)
) and
\mathit{iface}(V)
implies

\lfloor\Delta;\Gamma\rfloor\vdash e^{\prime}:\lfloor T@V\rfloor

Proof.

By simultaneous induction on the definition of compilation. Statements (3)
and (4) above follow immediately by i.h.(2). We often
make implicit use of Lemmas
9.27
and
9.28
above
for conciseness.

Case::

var
and
const

Trivial.

Case::

select-boxed

\vdash e.f\mapsto e^{\prime}.i.(\lfloor S_{i}\rfloor@\lfloor V_{i}\rfloor)
and
\neg\mathit{tyvar}(S_{i})

this case

\vdash e.f:S_{i}
by inversion on typing

\vdash e:T
and
\mathit{struct}(T)

and

by

9.4.
Behavioural equivalence

Definition 9.30 (Lowering Simulation).

Given a WG program
\overline{D}\rhd e
and a LWG program
\overline{D^{\dagger}}\rhd d
.
A relation
\mathcal{R}
is a
lowering simulation
when for all
(e,d)\in\mathcal{R}

•

if
e
is a value then
e\multimap d
,

•

if
e\not\longrightarrow
then
d\not\longrightarrow
, and

•

if
e\longrightarrow e^{\prime}
then there is
d^{\prime}
such that
d\longrightarrow^{\ast}d^{\prime}
and
(e^{\prime},d^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

Equivalence for values
e\multimap d

\frac{v\multimap v^{\prime}S\prec:_{\emptyset}T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.77498pt}{{:}}}_{\emptyset}V}{v\multimap{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{T}@{V}}(S,\rho,v^{\prime})}\par\frac{\overline{v\multimap v^{\prime}}T\prec:_{\emptyset}U}{T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\multimap{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\par\frac{T\prec:_{\emptyset}U}{T(c)\multimap U(c)}

Figure 21.
Correspondence between WG and LWG values.

Equivalence modulo synthetic casts
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d_{1}\bumpeq d_{2}
with
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d_{i}:\Phi_{i}

\frac{\,}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq d}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{refl}\par\frac{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d^{\prime}\bumpeq d}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{sym}\par\frac{T\prec:_{\Delta}U}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash T(c)\bumpeq U(c)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{base}\par\frac{T\prec:_{\Delta}U\overline{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}}}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}T}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{d}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{d^{\prime}}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{stru}\par\frac{\,}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash w\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(S,\rho,w)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{ifaceval}\par\par\frac{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq w}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}U}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}T}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}@}{V})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{T}@{V}}(S,\rho,w)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{ifacevalchange}\par\frac{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq w}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d.({T}@{V})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{T}@{V}}(S,\rho,w)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{ifacevalassert}\par\frac{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq w}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d.({S}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{T}@{S}}(S,\rho,w)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{ifacevalmake}\par\frac{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}.({V}@{T})^{\circ}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{dassert}\par\frac{\ \Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}}{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq Figure 22.
Equivalence between LWG
well-typed
expressions modulo synthetic casts.
We write
d\bumpeq d^{\prime}
for
\emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}
and
we write
{e.({V}@{T})^{\circ}}
to indicate a type-assert added by the compiler.

Lemma 9.31.

Given a WG program
\overline{D}\rhd e
and its compilation
\overline{D^{\dagger}}\rhd d
.
For all types
S
and
T
defined in
\overline{D}
, the following holds:

•

If
S\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}T\iff\lfloor S\rfloor\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}\lfloor T\rfloor
.

•

If
S\prec:_{\Delta}T\iff\lfloor S\rfloor\prec:_{\Delta}\lfloor T\rfloor
.

Proof.

Straightforward from the definitions of
\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}
,
\prec:
, and

\lfloor\_\rfloor
. Note that
\lfloor\_\rfloor
renames consistently each
method
m
in interfaces to
m_{D}
(incl. anonymous types) and, for
each method
m
defined on non-interface types, an additional
adaptor method
m_{D}
is defined.
∎

Lemma 9.32 (Type pairs).

For any
e
such that

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e:{S}@{T}
,

•

if
\mathit{iface}(S)\implies\mathit{iface}(T)
, and

•

if
\neg\mathit{iface}(T)\implies S=T
.

Proof.

Straightforward from the judgement
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e:{S}@{T}
.
∎

Lemma 9.33.

Suppose
\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
.
Let
v
be a value such that
\emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash v:{T}@{V}

•

\mathit{iface}(V)
then
v={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{T}@{V}}(S,\rho,e)
,
\emptyset;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mathbin{:}{S}@{S}
,

S\prec:_{\emptyset}T\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\emptyset}V

•

\neg\mathit{iface}(V)
then
v=T\{w\}
or
v=T(c)
and
V=T

Proof.

Immediate by inversion from
\emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash v:{T}@{V}
(Figure
11
).
∎

Lemma 9.34.

Suppose
\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e:U
.
If
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mapsto d

and

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e:U\mapsto_{\Psi}d^{\prime}

then
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Straightforward case analysis on the judgement of the form

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e:U\mapsto_{\Psi}d^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 9.35.

Suppose

\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and

\Delta;\,\overline{x:U}\vdash e\mathbin{:}T
and

\Delta;\,\emptyset\vdash\overline{v\mathbin{:}U}
.
Assume
\Delta;\,\overline{x:U}\vdash e\mapsto e^{\prime}
and

\Delta;\,\emptyset\vdash\overline{v\mapsto d}
.
Then

\Delta;\,\emptyset\vdash e[\overline{x:=v}]\mapsto e^{\prime}[\overline{x:=d}]
.

Proof.

Mechanical by investigating the type derivation of

\Delta;\,\overline{x:U}\vdash e\mathbin{:}T
with
Theorem
9.29
.
∎

Lemma 9.36.

Suppose
\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and we have

\mathit{body}(t[\overline{R^{\prime}}].m[\overline{S^{\prime}}])=(x,\overline{y}).e
in WG
such that

\texttt{\bf func}~(x~t[\overline{\alpha~R}])~m[\overline{\beta~S}](\overline{y~{T}@{T^{\prime}}})~{V}@{V^{\prime}}~\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\bf return}~e^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\in\overline{D}\;\text{and}\;\emptyset;\,x:t[\overline{\alpha}],\overline{y:T}\vdash e\mathbin{:}V\mapsto d

and

\mathit{body_{L}}(t.m)=[\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta}](x,\overline{y}).d^{\prime}

in LWG.

Then we have
\emptyset;\,x:t[\overline{\alpha}],\overline{y:T}\vdash d\bumpeq d^{\prime}[\overline{\alpha:=\lfloor R\rfloor}][\overline{\beta:=\lfloor S^{\prime}\rfloor}]
.

Proof.

Straightforward application of Rule
d-func
and Lemma
9.34
.
∎

Lemma 9.37.

Suppose
\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
.
If

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mathbin{:}T
,
and

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mapsto d
,
for any context
E
, such that
\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash E[e]\mathbin{:}V
,
there exists an LWG
context
E^{\prime}
and term
d^{\prime}
such that:

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash E[e]\mapsto E^{\prime}[d^{\prime}]

and

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash d^{\prime}\bumpeq d
.

Proof.

By structural induction on the derivation of

\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash e\mapsto d
.
∎

Lemma 9.38.

If
\delta(\circ,\overline{v})=v^{\prime}
with

\emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash\overline{v}:T
in WG.
Then
for any
\overline{w}
such that
\overline{v\multimap w}
and
\delta(\circ_{T},\overline{w})=w^{\prime}
, we have
v^{\prime}\multimap w^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that operators only take base types (or
named based types) as operand.
∎

Lemma 9.39.

Suppose
\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
.
Let
v
be a WG value s.t.
\vdash v\mathbin{:}T
. Then following holds:

(1)

If
\vdash v\mapsto e
then
e\longrightarrow^{\ast}v^{\prime}
, where
v^{\prime}

is an LWG value s.t.
v\multimap v^{\prime}
and
e\bumpeq v^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
\vdash v\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{V}}e
and

T\prec:_{\emptyset}U\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\emptyset}V
then
e\longrightarrow^{\ast}v^{\prime}
,
where
v^{\prime}
is an LWG value s.t.
v\multimap v^{\prime}
and

e\bumpeq v^{\prime}
.

(3)

If

\vdash v\mathbin{:}T\Mapsto_{{U}@{V}}e
and

T\prec:_{\emptyset}U\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\emptyset}V
then
e\longrightarrow^{\ast}v^{\prime}
,
where
v^{\prime}
is an LWG value s.t.
v\multimap v^{\prime}
and

e\bumpeq v^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on the structure of
v
.

Base cases.

•

v=T(c)

For (1) by Rule
Const
, we have

\vdash T(c)\mapsto T(c)
, hence we have the result with

e=v^{\prime}=T(c)
, and
e\bumpeq v^{\prime}
, trivially.

For (2) since

T
is not an interface, it can only be derived
from

–

Rule
static-change
.
We have
\vdash T(c)\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{U}}e
.
This gives us
e=T(c).({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor})
.
where we have
\lfloor U\rfloor=U
and
{\lfloor T\rfloor}=T
since
they cannot be literal/anonymous types.

Thus we have
e\longrightarrow U(c)=v^{\prime}
, an LWG value, and

v\multimap v^{\prime}
since
T\prec:U
.

Finally, we have

e=T(c).({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{T}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}})\bumpeq U(c)=v^{\prime}
easily
by Figure
22
.

–

Rule
make-iface
.
We have

\vdash T(c)\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{V}}e
with

\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\mathit{iface}(U)
.
Pose
\rho=\mathsf{mkTable}({T},V)
, we have

e=T(c).({{T}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{{U}@{V}}{}}})
.

Thus we have

e\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}@{V}}(T,\rho,T(c))=v^{\prime}
, an LWG
value and
v\multimap v^{\prime}
.

Finally, we have

e=T(c).({{T}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{{U}@{V}}{}}})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}@{V}}(T,\rho,T(c))=v^{\prime}

easily
by Figure
22
.

–

Rule
make-bs
.
We have

\vdash T(c)\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{V}}e
with

\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
.
Pose
\rho=\mathsf{mkTable}({T},V)
, we have

e=T(c).({{U}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{{U}@{V}}{}}})
.

Thus we have

e\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}@{V}}(U,\rho,T(c))=v^{\prime}
, an LWG
value and
v\multimap v^{\prime}
.

Finally, we have

e=T(c).({{U}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{{U}@{V}}{}}})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}@{V}}(U,\rho,T(c))=v^{\prime}

easily
by Figure
22
.

For (3), if
\mathit{iface}(V)
, then it reduces to case (2). If

\neg\mathit{iface}(V)
then we have

\vdash v\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{\textit{Any}}}e
and

T\prec:U\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}\textit{Any}
, thus case (2) applies again.

•

v=T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}

For (1) we have
\vdash T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
.
Thus
e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}=v^{\prime}
and it is already a
value and we have
v\multimap v^{\prime}
and
e\bumpeq v^{\prime}
trivially.

For (2) since
T
is not an interface, it can only be derived from

–

Rule
static-change
.
We have
\vdash T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{U}}e
.
This gives us
e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor})
.

Thus we have
e\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}=v^{\prime}
, an LWG value
and
v\multimap v^{\prime}
since
T\prec:U
.

Finally, we have

e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}=v^{\prime}

easily
by Figure
22
.

–

Rule
make-iface
.
We have

\vdash T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{V}}e
with

\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\mathit{iface}(U)
.
Pose
\rho=\mathsf{mkTable}({T},V)
, we have

e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor})
.

Thus we have

e\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}(T,\rho,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})=v^{\prime}
, an
LWG value and
v\multimap v^{\prime}
.

Finally, we have

e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor},\rho,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})=v^{\prime}
easily by
Figure
22
.

–

Rule
make-bs
.
We have

\vdash T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{V}}e
with

\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\neg\mathit{iface}(U)
.
Pose
\rho=\mathsf{mkTable}({T},V)
, we have

e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({{\lfloor U\rfloor}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}{}}})
.

Thus we have

e\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}({\lfloor U\rfloor},\rho,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})=v^{\prime}
, an LWG
value and
v\multimap v^{\prime}
.

Finally, we have

e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({{{\lfloor U\rfloor}}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}{}}})\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}({\lfloor U\rfloor},\rho,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})=v^{\prime}

easily
by Figure
22
.

Inductive cases.

Assume the lemma holds for all substructures of a struct value. We
need to prove it holds for
v=T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
.

For (1), by Rule
struct
, we have

\vdash T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, where
\vdash v_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for each
i
.
By the induction hypothesis, each
e_{i}\longrightarrow^{\ast}v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
where

v^{\prime}_{i}
is an LWG value and
v_{i}\multimap v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
.
Therefore,

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\longrightarrow^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime\prime}_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, an LWG value, through several
applications of the
context
rule.
By definition of
\multimap
, we also have

T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\multimap{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime\prime}_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
.
By induction hypothesis, we also have
e_{i}\bumpeq v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
for each

i
, hence, we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\bumpeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime\prime}_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
.

For (2) since
T
is not an interface, it can only be derived from

•

Rule
static-change
. We have

\vdash T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{U}}e
.
This gives us

e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{e}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor})
.
where
v_{i}:T_{i}\mapsto_{\Phi_{i}}e_{i}
for each
i
.

By the induction hypothesis, each
e_{i}\longrightarrow^{\ast}v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, where

v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is an LWG value and
v_{i}\multimap v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
and
e_{i}\bumpeq v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
.

Thus we have
e\longrightarrow^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v^{\prime\prime}}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor})\longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v^{\prime\prime}}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}=v^{\prime}

an LWG value
and
v\multimap v^{\prime}
by IH and the fact that
T\prec:U
.

Finally, we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{e}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor}){}\bumpeq{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor U\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v^{\prime\prime}}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}
by IH, Figure
22
, and the fact that
T\prec:U
.

•

Rule
make-iface
.
We have

\vdash T\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\mathbin{:}T\mapsto_{{U}@{V}}e
with

\mathit{iface}(V)
and
\mathit{iface}(U)
.
Pose
\rho=\mathsf{mkTable}({T},V)
, we have

e={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{e}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({{T}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}{}}})

where
v_{i}:T_{i}\mapsto_{\Phi_{i}}e_{i}
for each
i
.

By the induction hypothesis, each
e_{i}\longrightarrow^{\ast}v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, where

v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is an LWG value and
v_{i}\multimap v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
and
e_{i}\bumpeq v^{\prime\prime}_{i}
.

Thus we have
e\longrightarrow^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v^{\prime\prime}}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.({{T}{}}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}{}}})\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{U}@{V}\rfloor}(T,\rho,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor T\rfloor}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v^{\prime\prime}}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace})=v^{\prime}
, an LWG value and
v\multimap v^{\prime}
by IH.

Finally, we have

by IH and Figure

.

Lemma 9.40.

Suppose
\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e:T
for some

T
.
Then, the relation

\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\,(e,d^{\prime})\,\mid\,\exists d.\ \emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e\mapsto d\text{ and }d\bumpeq d^{\prime}\,\}

is a lowering simulation.

Proof.

We show that

\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\,(e,d^{\prime})\,\mid\,\exists d.\ \emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e\mapsto d\text{ and }d\bumpeq d^{\prime}\,\}

is a lowering simulation. We proceed by induction on the structure of
the reduction.

Figure
24
gives an overview of our proof
strategy.
We start from
e
, which evaluates (one-step) to
e^{\prime}
and compiles to
d
, and assume there is
d^{\prime}

such that
d\bumpeq d^{\prime}
,
then we show that there is
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that
d^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{\ast}d^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
with
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
a compilation target from
e^{\prime}
, possibly via a

\bumpeq
-equivalent term (
d^{\prime\prime}
); hence we have that

(e^{\prime},d^{\prime\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

e
e^{\prime}
d
d^{\prime\prime}
d^{\prime}
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
\longrightarrow
\longrightarrow^{\ast}
\mapsto
\mapsto
d\bumpeq d^{\prime}
d^{\prime\prime}\bumpeq d^{\prime\prime\prime}

Figure 23.
Key diagram for the proof of Lemma
9.40
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}d=d^{\prime}&=&n{\#}t[\overline{\lfloor R\rfloor}].m_{D}[\overline{\lfloor S\rfloor}](\overline{n^{\prime}}).({\lfloor U[\eta]\rfloor}@{\lfloor U[\eta]\rfloor})\\

&=&v^{\prime}.t[\overline{\lfloor R\rfloor}]{\#}m[\overline{\lfloor S^{\prime}\rfloor}](\overline{d}).({\lfloor V\rfloor}\rightarrow_{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{\lfloor{V}@{\texttt{Any}}\rfloor}}).({\lfloor U[\eta]\rfloor}@{\lfloor U[\eta]\rfloor})\\
&&\qquad\qquad\text{with }\neg\mathit{iface}(T^{\prime}_{i})\implies d_{i}=w^{\prime}_{i}.({\lfloor T_{i}\rfloor}@{\lfloor T_{i}\rfloor})\text{ and }\mathit{iface}(T^{\prime}_{i})\implies d_{i}=w^{\prime}_{i}\\

&&\qquad\text{since}:w^{\prime}_{i}.({\lfloor T_{i}\rfloor}@{\lfloor T_{i}\rfloor})={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor{T_{i}}@{\textit{Any}}\rfloor}(T_{i},\emptyset,w^{\prime\prime}_{i})({\lfloor T_{i}\rfloor}@{\lfloor T_{i}\rfloor})\longrightarrow w^{\prime\prime}_{i}\text{ and }w^{\prime}_{i}\bumpeq w^{\prime\prime}_{i}\\
&\longrightarrow&e^{\prime}_{1}[\overline{\alpha:=\lfloor R\rfloor}][\overline{\beta:=\lfloor S^{\prime}\rfloor}][x:=v^{\prime}][\overline{y:=w^{\prime}}].({\lfloor U\rfloor}\rightarrow_{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{\lfloor{U}@{\texttt{Any}}\rfloor}}).({\lfloor U[\eta]\rfloor}@{\lfloor U[\eta]\rfloor})=d^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{array}

Assume

e^{\prime}\mapsto d^{\prime\prime}
,
we have
d^{\prime\prime}\bumpeq d^{\prime\prime\prime}
from Lemmas
9.35
and
9.36
.

–

The case for Rule

is similar to above
with the synthetic cast

instead of

.

•

If

, with

.
Assume
\emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash\overline{v}:T
.

–

Rule
t-op-param
cannot have applied here (we cannot have

with
e:\alpha
as terms are closed).w

–

With Rule

.

By Lemma

, we have

and

.

.

as required.

, with

.

{\lfloor T\rfloor}=T
and
{\lfloor U\rfloor}

and we have

, as required.
The case when

Definition 9.41 (Lifting Simulation).

Given a WG program
\overline{D}\rhd e
and a LWG program
\overline{D^{\dagger}}\rhd d
.
A relation
\mathcal{R}
is a
lifting simulation
when for all
(d,e)\in\mathcal{R}

•

if
d
is a value then
e\multimap d
,

•

if
d\not\longrightarrow
then
e\not\longrightarrow
, and

•

if
d\longrightarrow d^{\prime}
then there is
e^{\prime}
such that
e\longrightarrow^{\ast}e^{\prime}
and
(d^{\prime},e^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

e
e^{\prime}
d^{\prime}
d^{\prime\prime}
d
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
\longrightarrow^{\ast}
\longrightarrow
\mapsto
\mapsto
\bumpeq
\bumpeq

Figure 24.
Key diagram for the proof of Lemma
9.42
.

Lemma 9.42.

Suppose
\overline{D}~\mathit{\checkmark}
and
\emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e:T
for some

T
.
Then, the relation

\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\,(d,e)\,\mid\,\exists d^{\prime}.\ \emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e\mapsto d^{\prime}\text{ and }d\bumpeq d^{\prime}\,\}

is a lifting simulation.

Proof.

We show that

\mathcal{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\,(d,e)\,\mid\,\exists d^{\prime}.\ \emptyset;\,\emptyset\vdash e\mapsto d^{\prime}\text{ and }d\bumpeq d^{\prime}\,\}

is a lifting simulation. We proceed by induction on the structure of
the LWG reduction.

Figure
9.42
gives an overview of our proof
strategy.
We start from
d
, which evaluates (one-step) to
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
. We show
that there must be a WG term
e
which must compile to an

\bumpeq
-equivalent term, i.e.,
d^{\prime}
.
We then show that
e
reduces (in zero or more steps) to
e^{\prime}
which
compiles to
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
, possibly via an
\bumpeq
-equivalent term,
i.e.,
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

•

If

d={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\lfloor S\rfloor}\{\overline{v}\}.i\rightarrow v_{i}=d^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Then we must show that
e
reduces to an equivalent value.
Then we must have
e=S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{w}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.f_{i}
with
\overline{w\multimap v}
.
By inversion, we must have

d^{\prime}=S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.f_{i}.(\Phi_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\downharpoonright_{1}})
(if
select-boxed
was
applied) or
d^{\prime}=S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.f_{i}
(if

select-unboxed
was applied);
assuming

\overline{f~\Phi}=\mathit{fields}_{\Delta}(S)

and

\overline{\Delta;\,\Gamma\vdash w\mathbin{:}U\mapsto_{\Phi}n}
, with
\overline{U\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}\Phi{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\downharpoonright_{1}}}
.
Via Lemma
9.39
, we have

S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.f_{i}\longrightarrow^{\ast}w_{i}\multimap v_{i}

and since
U_{i}\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\Delta}\Phi_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\downharpoonright_{1}}
, we also have

S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.f_{i}.(\Phi_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\downharpoonright_{1}})\longrightarrow^{\ast}w_{i}.(\Phi_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\downharpoonright_{1}})\longrightarrow w_{i}\multimap v_{i}

as required.

•

d=w.({\lfloor S\rfloor}\rightarrow_{\rho}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi})\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w)=d^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
Here we have that
w=\lfloor S\rfloor(c)
or

w=\lfloor S\rfloor\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
, and
d^{\prime}
can be any term

\bumpeq
-equivalent to
w
(i.e.,
w
with additional
synthetic casts).
Hence, from the compilation rules, it must be the case that

e=S(c)
or
e=S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
e=e^{\prime}
, and
d=d^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus the case follows trivially with

e\multimap{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w)
.

•

d={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w).({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}T}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}T^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}@}{U})\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{T^{\prime}}@{U}}}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho^{\prime},w)=d^{\prime\prime\prime}

with
\rho^{\prime}=\mathsf{mkTable}_{\emptyset}(\lfloor S\rfloor,U)
.
Hence, from the compilation rules, it must be the case that

e=S(c)
or
e=S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
e=e^{\prime}
, and
d=d^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus the case follows trivially with

e\multimap{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w)
.

•

Cases for Rules
static-change-s
and

static-change-c
are similar
to the previous two cases.

•

d={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w).(\lfloor S\rfloor)\rightarrow w=d^{\prime\prime\prime}
, via Rule
assert-ok
S
.
We must have
w=\lfloor S\rfloor(c)
or
w=\lfloor S\rfloor\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
and
d^{\prime}
can be any term
\bumpeq
-equivalent to
w
(i.e.,
w

with additional synthetic casts).
There are two cases:

–

e=S(c)
or
e=S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
,
e=e^{\prime}
, and
d=d^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus the case follows trivially with

e\multimap{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w).({\lfloor S\rfloor}@{\lfloor S\rfloor})
.

–

If we have
e=v^{\prime}.(S)
with
v^{\prime}=S(c)
or

v^{\prime}=S\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\overline{v}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}
., then
we have
e^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
, and thus the case follows
trivially with
v^{\prime}\multimap w
.

•

d={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{U}@{V^{\prime}}}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho^{\prime},w).(U^{\prime})\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}{{U^{\prime}}@{U^{\prime}}}}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w)=d^{\prime\prime\prime}
, with

\lfloor S\rfloor\mathbin{\texttt{<}\mkern-1.0mu\raisebox{0.86108pt}{{:}}}_{\emptyset}U^{\prime}
and
\rho=\mathsf{mkTable}_{\emptyset}(\lfloor S\rfloor,U^{\prime})

via Rule
assert-ok
I
.
There are two cases:

–

or

,

, and

.
Thus the case follows easily with

e\multimap{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(\lfloor S\rfloor,\rho,w).(U^{\prime})
.

–

If we have
e=v^{\prime}.(\lfloor U^{\prime}\rfloor^{-1})
with
v^{\prime}=S(c)
or

, then the WG transition fires since we have

(Lemma
9.31
).
Thus we have
e^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
, and thus the case follows
trivially with
v^{\prime}\multimap w
.

•

d=w{\#}t[\overline{\lfloor R\rfloor}].m[\overline{\lfloor S\rfloor}](\overline{v})\rightarrow e[\overline{\alpha:=\lfloor R\rfloor}][\overline{\beta:=\lfloor S\rfloor}][x:=v][\overline{y:=v}]

with

[\overline{\alpha};\,\overline{\beta}](x,\overline{y}).e=\mathit{body_{L}}(t.m)
.
Then we must have

with
\overline{v^{\prime}\multimap v}
.
Posing

and

.

•

d={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0.5}\Phi}(U,\rho,v).m[\overline{S}](\overline{v})\rightarrow e[\overline{\alpha:=R}][\overline{\beta:=S}][x:=v][\overline{y:=v}]=d^{\prime}
with

Following Rule
d-func

here,

•

•

d=E[n]\rightarrow E[m]=d^{\prime}
with
n\longrightarrow m
.
By induction hypothesis on
n\longrightarrow m
, we have

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash n^{\prime\prime}\mapsto n^{\prime}\bumpeq n
; and

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash m^{\prime\prime}\mapsto m^{\prime}\bumpeq m
;
and
n^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow^{\ast}m^{\prime\prime}
.
Since compilation preserve the structure of terms, we must have
E^{\prime}
such that
